**One refinement is needed.** A row whose
series letter is B or later is evidence that its design number existed before the row, whatever the
register carries, **because the series letter counts models of a number that was already approved.**
Without that filter the walk would count the X-40B as a new design number, **and so misread the register's edge as a government decision.**

**With the filter the research series gives 23 new design numbers over
22 steps, of which fifteen are exactly one.** The unmanned
series gives 30 over 29 steps with nine exactly one. The share of increments equal to one is

$$
\theta_{\Delta} \;=\; \frac{\lvert \{\, k : \Delta_k = 1 \,\} \rvert}{\lvert \{\, k \,\} \rvert},
\qquad
\theta_{\Delta}^{X} \;=\; \frac{15}{22} \;=\; 68.2\,\%,
\qquad
\theta_{\Delta}^{Q} \;=\; \frac{9}{29} \;=\; 31.0\,\%
$$

### The Increment and the Pointer Measure Different Things, and the Article Needs Both

**An increment of one and a pointer advancing by one are not the same statement, and conflating them
double-counts.** Write the running maximum as the pointer, the quantity the allocation
procedure reads, and define its advance.

$$
\nu_k \;=\; \max_{\,j \le k} d_j
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\alpha_k \;=\; \nu_k - \nu_{k-1}
$$

**The two differ exactly when a number below the pointer is allocated**, and there the identity is
immediate.

$$
\alpha_k \;=\; 0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
d_k \;\le\; \nu_{k-1}
$$

**An allocation below the pointer makes its increment negative and inflates the next one by the same amount**,
so counting skips off the increment counts each out-of-order allocation twice. The pointer does
not have that defect, and the numbers actually skipped are the advances in excess of one.

$$
\lvert A_{\mathrm{skip}} \rvert \;=\; \sum_{k \,:\, \alpha_k > 1} \bigl(\alpha_k - 1\bigr)
$$

**For the research series that sum is eleven, across five advances greater than one,
with sixteen advances of exactly one and one of zero, over 22 steps.** The
obedience rate is therefore the share of advances equal to one.

$$
\theta \;=\; \frac{\lvert \{\, k : \alpha_k = 1 \,\} \rvert}{\lvert \{\, k \,\} \rvert}
$$

**That is 72.7 percent for the research series and 50.4 percent across the whole
register**, being 67 advances of one in 133 steps, with
43 allocations below the pointer.

**And the research series is the best-behaved sequence in the register.** That sharpens the finding.
Among the three basic missions with at least twenty steps the
rates are 72.7 percent for the X series over 22 steps, 51.5 percent for the M series over 33 steps and 44.8 percent for the Q series over 29 steps.

$$
\theta^{X} \;=\; \frac{16}{22} \;=\; 72.7\,\%,
\qquad
\theta^{M} \;=\; \frac{17}{33} \;=\; 51.5\,\%,
\qquad
\theta^{Q} \;=\; \frac{13}{29} \;=\; 44.8\,\%
$$

**The X-67 was lost from the one numbering sequence in this register that mostly does follow the
rule.**

### Every Research-Series Skip Has a Cause This Series Has Written About

**The five advances greater than one are not a statistical tail. They are a list, and the list is this series' table of contents.**

| After | Allocated | Date | Numbers skipped | What this series says happened |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 48 | X-50A | 2002-02-13 | 49 | the number was passed over so that a rotorcraft could have the round fifty, and the [X-49][related_post_a346_piasecki_x49] article records it |
| 51 | X-53A | 2006-08-16 | 52 | the [X-52][related_post_a349_x52_designation_refused] was requested and refused for resembling a bomber |
| 57 | X-59A | 2018-06-21 | 58 | the [X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] was consumed by an unmanned allocation |
| 66 | X-68A | 2025-08-20 | 67 | this article |
| 68 | X-76A | 2025-10-20 | 69 to 75 | the block leapfrogged for a number chosen as a nod to 1776 |

**And the one advance of zero is the one number that came back.** The X-49A was allocated on 2003-05-23,
464 days after the X-50A had passed it, filling a gap the pointer had already crossed
\[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]]. **So the register contains exactly
one research-series number that was skipped and later recovered**, and why that number could be recovered and the X-67 cannot is the subject of The Rule
Changed, and the Register Holds the Before-Case in the Same Series.

### Six Pairs of Numbers in the Register Carry the Same Official Sentence

**If the design number were an ordinal over designs, two different numbers should not describe the same
design.** The register can be asked. Stripping the note references, which differ between two rows by
construction, the register's 532 well-formed rows carry 490 distinct
descriptions of twenty characters or more. **Sixteen of those descriptions are shared by more than
one designation, and six of the shared ones span more than one design number**, the
other ten being series letters of a single number, the use series letters exist
for.

**Those six are all adjacent pairs, and there are no others.**

| Designations | Allocated | Contractors | The description both carry |
|---|---|---|---|
| C-145A and C-146A | 16-May-12 and 18-May-12 | PZL Mielec, Fairchild-Dornier | a twin-engine turboprop short takeoff and landing transport supporting airlift of passengers and cargo |
| MGM-165A and MGM-166A | 17-Jul-02 and 07-Oct-02 | Lockheed Martin, Lockheed Martin | a guided hypervelocity missile for close combat heavy armour and tank destruction |
| X-46A and X-47A | 14-May-01 and 14-May-01 | Boeing, Northrop Grumman | aircraft carrier based to demonstrate feasibility of unmanned combat air vehicle |
| YAGM-180A and YAGM-181A | 02-Jun-16 and 02-Jun-16 | Lockheed Martin, Raytheon | long range standoff cruise missile prototype |
| LS-15A and LS-16A | 09-Sep-11 and 17-Jan-12 | Ball Aerospace, Lockheed Martin | launch detection and space surveillance satellite |
| X-63A and X-64A | 20-Apr-22 and 20-Apr-22 | ABL Space Systems, Invocon with KT Engineering and Troy7 | demonstrator rocket for the laboratory's Aerospike Rocket Integration and Suborbital Experiment programme |

**In five of the six the contractor is the only field that differs, and
three of the six were allocated on the same day.** In the remaining
one the contractor is the same firm and the register offers nothing to
distinguish the two numbers. **Two of the six are research-series pairs**, a third
of the set from one basic mission out of twenty, and this series has already written about both
of them \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]] \[[X-Planes: Invocon X-64][related_post_a361_invocon_x64]].

**The reading of that table is narrow but sufficient.** The official statement of purpose does not
distinguish these pairs, so whatever distinguishes two design numbers in the register's practice is not
the stated purpose. **In five of six cases it is the designing organisation.**

### The Two Candidate Readings Are an Injectivity and a Well-Definedness, and Both Fail

**Stating the two readings as properties of maps makes the failures precise and makes them countable.**
Let $\mathcal{N}$ be the set of mission-and-number pairs the register carries, and let the register
supply two maps out of it.

$$
\mathsf{purpose} : \mathcal{N} \to \mathcal{T}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\mathsf{maker} : \mathcal{N} \to \mathcal{F}
$$

**The first reading, that a design number indexes a purpose, requires the purpose map to be
injective.** Two different numbers must not carry the same official statement.

$$
\mathsf{purpose}(x) = \mathsf{purpose}(y) \;\Longrightarrow\; x = y
$$

**It is not injective and the failures are counted.** Over 490 distinct descriptions of
twenty characters or more, **six are shared by more than one design number.**

**The second reading, that a design number indexes an organisation, requires the maker map to be well
defined**, a weaker demand than injectivity and a different one. It asks only that every row on
one number name the same firm.

$$
\lvert \{\, \mathsf{maker}(w) \;:\; w \in \mathcal{N}^{-1}(x) \,\} \rvert \;=\; 1
\qquad\text{for every } x \in \mathcal{N}
$$

**It is not well defined either.** Of 235 mission-and-number pairs,
**27 carry rows naming more than one leading firm, or 11.5 percent.**

**The two readings fail in different ways, and the article states both without choosing between them.** The
purpose map is a function and is not injective, so **a number carries no more purpose than its
neighbour.** The maker map is not even a function, so **a number does not carry a maker at all.**

### And a Design Number Can Survive a Change of Manufacturer, So the Pairing Was a Choice

**If the design number tracked the designer, a change of designer would force a new number, and it does
not.** Of the register's design numbers, 103 carry more than one row. **36 of
those carry more than one distinct contractor string and 27 carry more than one distinct
leading firm name.**

**That last count supports two shares, and each is given with its denominator.**

$$
\frac{\lvert \mathcal{M} \rvert}{\lvert \mathcal{N}_{>1} \rvert} \;=\; 26.2\,\%
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\frac{\lvert \mathcal{M} \rvert}{\lvert \mathcal{N} \rvert} \;=\; 11.5\,\%
$$

**The first is as a share of the 103 numbers that carry more than one row, which is the
population in which a change of firm is even possible. The second is as a share of all
235 numbers**, the figure the well-definedness condition above reports.

**The clearest case, and an extreme one, is a missile rather than an aeroplane.** The design number 88 in
the missile series carries ten rows across the series letters A, D, E, E, E, F, G, G, H and J, and the
contractor field across them names seven distinct leading firms, among them Texas Instruments,
Raytheon, Northrop Grumman and a naval weapons centre
\[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]]. **One design number, ten series letters, and a succession
of primes spanning decades.**

**The system can hold one design number across a change of manufacturer.** The X-46A and the
X-47A could have been the X-46A and the X-46B, and the X-63A and X-64A the X-63A and X-63B.
**They were not, and the choice was available.** The design number indexes neither the stated purpose nor
the designing organisation reliably. **It indexes a request, and the instruction leaves undefined what a request is for.**

### A Chosen Number Carries Information and a Sequential One Carries None

**This is the quantitative reason a skipped number matters at all, and it can be stated exactly.** Let
$\nu_k$ be the number a sequential allocator would issue at the moment of the $k$th allocation, which
by the procedure is one more than the last approved design number of that basic mission.

$$
\nu_k \;=\; 1 + \max_{\,j < k} d_j
$$

**If every allocation takes the next number then $d_k = \nu_k$ for all $k$, the sequence is a deterministic function of its history, and a designation carries no information beyond its order.**
The
quantity that carries anything is the departure.

$$
\delta_k \;=\; d_k - \nu_k
$$

**An in-sequence allocation has $\delta_k = 0$.** The information a chosen number can carry is bounded
by the logarithm of the count of positions available to it, which runs from the sequential number to the
top of the field.

$$
I(\nu) \;=\; \log_2\!\bigl(N_d - \nu + 1\bigr)
$$

**The zero is better written as a conditional entropy than asserted**, because in that form
it describes the register, where an assertion would describe one allocation.

$$
H\bigl(d_k \,\big|\, d_1, \ldots, d_{k-1}\bigr) \;=\; 0
\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad
d_k = \nu_{k-1} + 1
$$

**At the research series' position in July 2023 that bound is 9.8657 bits, against
9.9643 bits for a number free to be anything.** The bound covers almost the entire field because the
field is almost empty. The earlier article made the same observation as an argument about
scarcity, and here it serves as an argument about expressiveness. **The system is not short of numbers, so a number
is cheap, so choosing one can afford to say something.**

**The saying happens in the unmanned series, and the departures are measurable.** Taking the
sequential number as the top of the unbroken opening run, the convention the compiler's
published figures follow, the out-of-sequence unmanned allocations that carry a full date have the
following departures.

| Designation | Allocated | Sequential number then | Departure |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| MQ-35A | 2023-04-24 | 30 | 5 |
| YFQ-48A | 2025-08-20 | 31 | 17 |
| XQ-58A | 2017-07-10 | 28 | 30 |
| XQ-67A | 2023-07-19 | 30 | 37 |
| XRQ-72A | 2017-03-20 | 28 | 44 |
| XRQ-73A | 2023-09-12 | 30 | 43 |

**The departures are 5 for the number 35, 17 for the number 48, 30 for the number 58, 37 for the number 67, 44 for the number 72 and 43 for the number 73, ranging from 5 to 44.** There are
six distinct values among six observations, so the empirical distribution is
uniform and its entropy is 2.5850 bits, which equals the maximum
2.5850 attainable at that sample size.

$$
H \;=\; -\sum_i \pi_i \log_2 \pi_i \;=\; \log_2 n \quad\text{when every observation is distinct}
$$

**That coincidence is a warning and not a result, and it is labelled as one.** With
six observations all distinct, the entropy estimate is pinned at its ceiling by the sample
size and says nothing about the underlying distribution. **The useful statement is the comparison of
regimes rather than the number.** A sequential series has zero bits of choice in every allocation. **The
unmanned series carries 69 rows and now has nine out-of-sequence design numbers against a
sequential ceiling of 31, being 35, 42, 44, 48, 58, 67, 72, 73 and 170, of which six carry a full date and
can be placed in time, being 35, 48, 58, 67, 72 and 73**, and the register's compiler has written of the fighter series that it has to all intents
and purposes stopped having a sequence at all
\[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. **The X-67 is one number lost in the
middle of that transition.**

**The transition has a size in bits and summing the bound over the departures gives it.**

$$
I_{\Sigma} \;=\; \sum_{k \,:\, \delta_k > 0} \log_2\!\bigl(N_d - \nu_k + 1\bigr)
$$

**That is 59.5355 bits across the six dated out-of-sequence unmanned allocations,
or 9.9226 bits each.** The figure is a ceiling and not a measurement of what was said, because
**a bound on what a channel could carry is not a reading of what went through it.** What it establishes
is the scale. **A sequence that chose every number freely would carry about ten bits per allocation and a
sequence that took the next one carries none**, and the unmanned series has moved some distance from the
second toward the first.

### The Borrowing Claim Is Testable and It Passes

**The compiler asserts that the 58 and the 67 came from the research series and gives no evidence for the
second.** The assertion has a testable consequence. **If a borrowed number is taken from a neighbouring
series' queue, it should equal that series' next number at the moment of the borrowing, and it should
equal no other series' next number**, because a claim that matched many series at once would be
worthless.

**The test was run against every basic mission in the register at each out-of-sequence unmanned allocation's date.**
There are twenty basic missions and six out-of-sequence
unmanned numbers that carry a full date.

| Number | Designation | Allocated | Basic missions for which it was next in series |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 35 | MQ-35A | 2023-04-24 | none |
| 48 | YFQ-48A | 2025-08-20 | none |
| 58 | XQ-58A | 2017-07-10 | the research series |
| 67 | XQ-67A | 2023-07-19 | the research series |
| 72 | XRQ-72A | 2017-03-20 | none |
| 73 | XRQ-73A | 2023-09-12 | none |

**The test fires two times in six, on the numbers 58 and 67, and both times
it names the research series and nothing else.** It fires zero times on more than one series. **It
is silent on four numbers, 35, 48, 72 and 73, which are exactly the four the compiler either
declines to derive or derives from somewhere other than a series queue.**

**A null model gives the coincidence a scale, and its reference class is stated here.**
Take the null to be that an out-of-sequence number is drawn uniformly from the two-digit integers above
the unmanned series' ceiling, the range 32 to 99 inclusive and so
68 values, written $B$. **At the six dates in question the mean number of distinct
next-in-series values falling inside that range is 3.3333, written $\bar{\tau}$**, so the
per-draw hit probability $\pi$ is 0.049020.

$$
\pi \;=\; \frac{\bar{\tau}}{B} \qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Pr\!\left[\,N_h \ge k\,\right] \;=\; \sum_{i=k}^{n} \binom{n}{i} \, \pi^{\,i} \, (1-\pi)^{\,n-i}
$$

**The expectation is the more legible quantity and it comes first.**

$$
\mathbb{E}\bigl[N_h\bigr] \;=\; n \, \pi
$$

**At six draws and a per-draw probability of 0.049020 that is 0.2941 accidental
hits expected**, against two observed. **At $n$ equal to six and $k$ equal to
two the tail probability is 0.03159, or about one chance in 31.7.** That is a weak signal on its own and the article
does not pretend otherwise. **It is not on its own.** Both hits name the same series, the compiler
independently states that series for both, and the research series independently shows a gap at both
numbers. **Four facts that could each be a coincidence are not four coincidences.**

**The test is silent on the 48 because the fighter numbers reach 117**, so the compiler's reading of it
as following the F-47 uses the most recently allocated number, as noted under Three Definitions of the
Next Number, Written Down \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]]. **For the
research series in July 2023 the highest allocated number, the top of the contiguous run and the most
recently allocated all gave 67**, so the X-67 case is clean and the 48 case is not.

### The Aeroplane Was Built to Deny the Premise

**Here the airframe earns its place in an article about a number.** The design number exists to mark a
major design change within a basic mission, so the system presumes that an aeroplane has a design
identity and that changes to it are either major or minor. **The programme that consumed the X-67 slot
existed to show that an aeroplane's identity is divisible.**

**The laboratory's words, quoted above, put it as a taxonomy of a genus with mission kits added to the
frame, and the contractor's put it as a first instance** \[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]]
\[[GA-ASI first flight release][ref_gaasi_firstflight]].

**The laboratory also states what the two species were meant to differ in, and that detail sharpens the collision.**
The sensing station was conceived as slower while carrying sensors, with longer
endurance. **The weapon station was conceived as faster and more manoeuvrable, with less endurance and
better range.**
**The programme wanted to design both and to establish how much of the two could be made common**
\[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]].

**The designation question and the engineering question are the same question asked by two institutions that cannot hear each other.**
The instruction asks whether a change is major enough to earn
a new design number. The programme asks how much of two aeroplanes can be the same aeroplane. **Neither
has a criterion the other would accept.**

### By the Instruction's Own Test a Species Belongs on a Series Letter

**The instruction's only concrete criterion for the lower level is the one the genus concept is designed
to defeat.** A series letter marks later models representing major modifications that significantly alter
the vehicle's systems, components, **or change the logistics support of the vehicle**
\[[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]].

**A shared chassis is built precisely so that the logistics support does not change across species.** The
laboratory's stated benefit is leveraging standard substructures and subsystems in the manner of an
automotive product line, a claim about shared spares, shared tooling and shared training as much
as about shared drawings \[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]]. **By the instruction's own test, two species on one genus differ less than the instruction's threshold for a series letter, let alone for a design number.**

**The register's practice points the same way.** The missile design number 88, described above, holds
ten series letters across seven leading firms, so the system tolerates very large change under one
number. **Yet the first species on this genus received a design
number of its own, taken from a different series.**

**And the system has shown in the same decade that it can extend itself, so the absence of any mechanism for a shared chassis is a choice rather than an oversight.**
The 2020 instruction's
summary of changes records one addition to the designator alphabet, **a status prefix `e` meaning
digitally developed, defined as aircraft that are engineered in a virtual environment**
\[[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]]. It is the only lowercase symbol in the scheme.

**In the same issue that cancelled the public list, the system added a letter for how an aeroplane was engineered and added nothing for whether it shares a chassis with another aeroplane.**
The genus and
species approach was already a laboratory programme with contracts when that instruction was signed
\[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]] \[[USAspending][ref_usaspending]].
**The system encoded the design environment and left design sharing unencoded.**

**The prediction is explicit and the record has not yet tested it.** If an off-board weapon station is
built on the same genus, the instruction's criterion and the register's practice both suggest it should be
a series letter on the number the first species holds. **The register's post-2018 behaviour, in which a
new programme name attracts a new number, suggests it will get its own.** The weapon station does not
appear in the register as last updated on 23 August 2026, so the question is open, and **this article states which way
it expects the answer to go and why it may be wrong.**

### What a Shared Chassis Does to a Part Count

**The commonality claim is quantitative and its simplest form is a part count.** Take a family of $S$
species, each of which would have $P$ parts if designed alone, and let $C$ of those parts be common
across the family. The family then holds the common parts once and the distinct parts $S$ times.

$$
U \;=\; C \;+\; S\,\bigl(P - C\bigr)
$$

**Against wholly bespoke designs, which hold $SP$ distinct parts, the ratio is the quantity that matters,
and writing the shared fraction as $\phi = C/P$ reduces it to two variables.**

$$
\rho \;=\; \frac{U}{S P} \;=\; \frac{1}{S} \;+\; \left(1 - \frac{1}{S}\right)\bigl(1 - \phi\bigr)
$$

**The limits are the test of the expression and both are right.** At $\phi = 1$ every part is shared and
$\rho = 1/S$, so a family of three species holds a third of the distinct parts, which is
0.3333. At $\phi = 0$ nothing is shared and $\rho = 1$. **At a shared fraction of three fifths the
ratio is 0.7000 for two species and 0.6000 for three.**

**The gradient is constant, so in this model the first shared part is worth as much as the last one.**

$$
\frac{\partial \rho}{\partial \phi} \;=\; -\left(1 - \frac{1}{S}\right)
$$

**At three species that is -0.6667**, so each percentage point of sharing removes two thirds of a
point of the distinct-part count. **That constancy is the model's weakness, and the penalty subsection below repairs it.**

**The relations in this section are the simplest defensible forms of a theory with a much larger
literature.** The modern treatment of modularity as a design strategy with an economic value is Baldwin and Clark's, whose
chapters on what modularity is and on the value of splitting and substitution set out the option-value
argument this section does not attempt \[[Baldwin and Clark, Design Rules Volume 1][research_baldwin_clark_2000]]
\[[What Is Modularity?][research_baldwin_clark_modularity]]
\[[The Value of Modularity, Splitting and Substitution][research_baldwin_clark_splitting]]. **Those three
are cited from their registry records and not from a reading**, as the reference definitions state,
and the rest of that literature sits in the survey's modularity cluster.

**That relation is about drawings, qualification and spares rather than about unit cost**, because a part
that exists once has to be designed once, tested once, catalogued once and stocked once. **It is also the
weakest form of the claim, because it counts parts rather than weighting them**, and the parts a family
finds easiest to share are not the ones that cost most to design.

### What It Does to a Unit Cost

**Commonality acts on unit cost through quantity, and the standard relation is a power law.** Let unit
cost fall with cumulative quantity $Q$ as a negative power, and let the progress ratio $r$ be the factor
by which unit cost falls when quantity doubles.

$$
c(Q) \;=\; c_1 \, Q^{-b} \qquad\text{with}\qquad 2^{-b} = r
\qquad\text{so}\qquad b \;=\; -\log_2 r
$$

**An eighty-five percent progress ratio gives $b$ equal to 0.23447 and a ninety percent ratio gives
0.15200.** Now split each aeroplane into a shared fraction and a bespoke fraction. **The step the argument turns on is that the two fractions are built at different quantities**,
and once it is
written down the rest is arithmetic.

$$
Q_{\mathrm{shared}} \;=\; S \, Q_{\mathrm{species}}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
Q_{\mathrm{bespoke}} \;=\; Q_{\mathrm{species}}
$$

**The bespoke fraction is built at the quantity of one species and gains nothing. The shared fraction is
built at $S$ times that quantity and slides down the curve by $S^{-b}$.** The two add in proportion.

$$
\kappa \;=\; \bigl(1 - \phi\bigr) \;+\; \phi \, S^{-b}
$$

**At a shared fraction of three fifths, three species and an eighty-five percent curve, $\kappa$ is
0.86375, a saving of 13.63 percent.** Two species give 0.91000 and a shared
fraction of four fifths gives 0.81833. **Writing the relation as a single slope makes every later
argument an argument about one negative number.**

$$
\kappa \;=\; 1 + a\,\phi \qquad\text{where}\qquad a \;=\; S^{-b} - 1 \;<\; 0
$$

**For three species on an eighty-five percent curve the slope $a$ is -0.22709, and for two
species it is -0.15000.** The gradient of the cost factor is the slope itself, because
this model is linear in the shared fraction.

$$
\frac{\partial \kappa}{\partial \phi} \;=\; a \;=\; S^{-b} - 1
$$

**At three species that is -0.22709.** Because $a$ is negative for every $S$ greater than one, this
model predicts that commonality always helps, monotonically. **But it does not help without limit, and the limit matters because it bounds every claim the programme could make.**

$$
\lim_{S \to \infty} a \;=\; -1
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\lim_{S \to \infty} \kappa \;=\; 1 - \phi
$$

**So no family, however large, saves more in unit cost than the fraction it shares.**

$$
\sup_{S} \bigl(1 - \kappa\bigr) \;=\; \phi
$$

**At a three-fifths shared fraction the ceiling is 60.00 percent at any family size**, against
13.63 percent reached at three species. **The ceiling also makes the inversion well posed.**
Asking how many species a stated saving needs means solving the cost factor for
$S$, and the solution exists only below the ceiling.

$$
S \;=\; \left(1 - \frac{1 - \kappa}{\phi}\right)^{-1/b}
$$

**A tenth of unit cost needs 2.176 species at a three-fifths shared fraction, a fifth needs
5.637, and three tenths needs 19.227.** The last figure is the useful one.
**A programme wanting a thirty percent unit-cost saving from commonality alone, at these parameters,
needs about nineteen species on one chassis**, and the programme that consumed the X-67 named two.

**And the monotonic conclusion is still false, for a reason the model omits.**

### The Omission Is the Penalty, and Adding It Changes the Answer Qualitatively

**A shared part is not a free part.** A structure sized for the heaviest species is too heavy for the
lightest. **A chassis sized for the fastest is too draggy for the most enduring.** **The laboratory's description of the two species it wanted, quoted above, names this trade**
\[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]]. **So write a penalty factor on the delivered cost
of capability and ask what happens.**

**Take the penalty proportional to the shared fraction first, because it is the obvious choice and it
gives a degenerate answer.**

$$
\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}} \;=\; \bigl(1 + a\,\phi\bigr)\bigl(1 + \eta\,\phi\bigr)
\;=\; 1 + (a + \eta)\,\phi + a\,\eta\,\phi^{2}
$$

**The curvature settles it in one line.**

$$
\frac{\partial^{2} \kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}}{\partial \phi^{2}} \;=\; 2\,a\,\eta
$$

**That is negative for every admissible pair, being -0.13625 at three species and a penalty
coefficient of 30 percent, so the quadratic is concave and its stationary point is a maximum
rather than a minimum.** The cheapest shared fraction is therefore at one
end of the interval or the other, and full commonality beats none exactly when the product at $\phi = 1$
falls below one.

$$
\bigl(1 + a\bigr)\bigl(1 + \eta\bigr) < 1
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\eta \;<\; \frac{-a}{1 + a}
$$

**For three species on an eighty-five percent curve that threshold is 29.38 percent, and for
two species 17.65 percent.** Below the threshold, share everything. Above it, share nothing.
**A model whose answer is always all or nothing is telling the reader about its linearity and not about aeroplanes.**

**A penalty growing as the square of the shared fraction is the better shape and it gives an interior
answer.** The first parts a family shares are the ones it costs least to share, so the marginal penalty
should rise rather than stay flat.

$$
\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}} \;=\; \bigl(1 + a\,\phi\bigr)\bigl(1 + \eta\,\phi^{2}\bigr)
$$

$$
\frac{\partial \kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}}{\partial \phi}
\;=\; 3\,a\,\eta\,\phi^{2} \;+\; 2\,\eta\,\phi \;+\; a \;=\; 0
$$

**That first-order condition is a quadratic in $\phi$ and its discriminant is the quantity that decides
whether an interior optimum exists at all.**

$$
\Theta \;=\; 4\eta^{2} - 12\,a^{2}\eta \;=\; 4\eta\bigl(\eta - 3a^{2}\bigr)
$$

$$
\phi^{\ast} \;=\; \frac{-2\eta + \sqrt{\Theta}}{6\,a\,\eta}
$$

**So an interior optimum exists only when the penalty coefficient clears a threshold set by the slope
alone.**

$$
\eta \;>\; \eta_{\ast} \;=\; 3 a^{2}
$$

**That is 15.47 percent for three species on an eighty-five percent curve and
6.75 percent for two.** The threshold rises with the family size, and its limit follows from
the slope's.

$$
\lim_{S \to \infty} \eta_{\ast} \;=\; 3
$$

**So a sufficiently large family tolerates a sharing penalty of 300 percent before an interior
optimum appears at all**, the formal version of the observation that commonality is worth more
the more species carry it.
Below that the minimum is the corner at full commonality.

**At a penalty coefficient of 30 percent the optimum is interior and the three cases separate
properly.** With $a = -0.22709$ at three species, the optimum and the two corners evaluate as follows.

$$
\phi^{\ast} \;=\; 0.4463,
\qquad
\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}(\phi^{\ast}) \;=\; \bigl(1 + a\,\phi^{\ast}\bigr)\bigl(1 + \eta\,\phi^{\ast 2}\bigr) \;=\; 0.95235,
\qquad
\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}(1) \;=\; \bigl(1 + a\bigr)\bigl(1 + \eta\bigr) \;=\; 1.00479
$$

The optimum shared fraction is 44.63 percent, at which the effective cost factor is
0.95235, a saving of 4.76 percent against sharing nothing. **Full commonality at
the same penalty gives 1.00479, which is 0.48 percent worse than sharing
nothing at all.** So at that penalty the interior optimum beats both corners and the corner the first
model would have chosen is the wrong one.

**Every number in the last three paragraphs uses assumed parameters and none of them is measured for this aeroplane.**
The shared fraction of the genus is not public, the sharing penalty is not public, and
the number of species is one. **What the relations establish is the shape of the argument and where it
fails**, the most a model fitted to a programme that has published no cost data can yield.

### Inverting the Break-Even When One Species Exists

**The programme's claim is about development cost rather than unit cost, and a separate break-even applies.**
Let a bespoke aircraft type cost $c_b$ to develop, let the genus cost $\mathcal{C}_g$ once and let each species on
it cost $c_s$.

$$
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{platform}} \;=\; \mathcal{C}_g + S\,c_s
\qquad\text{against}\qquad
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{bespoke}} \;=\; S\,c_b
$$

$$
S^{\ast} \;=\; \frac{\mathcal{C}_g}{c_b - c_s}
$$

**That relation cannot be evaluated here, because $c_b$ is unknown and $S$ is one.** Inverting it is the
only direction the data supports.

$$
c_b \;=\; c_s + \frac{\mathcal{C}_g}{S}
$$

**Reading the genus cost as the platform-sharing programme's total of 24,479,631.19 dollar and the first species at the Off-Board Sensing Station award of 67,986,112.00 dollar, the figures follow.**

| Species the chassis must carry | Bespoke cost needed in dollar | Premium over the first species |
|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 | 80,225,927.59 | 18.00 percent |
| 3 | 76,145,989.06 | 12.00 percent |
| 5 | 72,882,038.24 | 7.20 percent |

$$
c_b\big|_{S=2} \;=\; 67{,}986{,}112.00 + \frac{24{,}479{,}631.19}{2} \;\approx\; 80{,}225{,}927.59,
\qquad
\frac{c_b - c_s}{c_s} \;=\; \frac{24{,}479{,}631.19}{2 \times 67{,}986{,}112.00} \;=\; 18.00\,\%
$$

**So a second species repays the chassis only if a bespoke demonstrator would have cost at least 80,225,927.59 dollar, which is 18.00 percent more than the first species cost.**
On the
narrower reading that only the winning firm's platform-sharing award of 10,674,331.00 dollar built the
chassis, the requirement falls to 73,323,277.50 dollar at two species, a premium of 7.85 percent,
and 71,544,222.33 dollar at three, a premium of 5.23 percent.

**The sensitivity of the break-even to the chassis cost is a reciprocal and it gives the figure a
programme manager would want.**

$$
\frac{\partial S^{\ast}}{\partial \mathcal{C}_g} \;=\; \frac{1}{c_b - c_s}
$$

**At the two-species figures above that is one additional species required for every
12,239,815.59 dollar added to the chassis.** The relation also states what the inversion shows,
**that the break-even is sensitive to a quantity nobody has published.**

**A third reading is that the chassis cost is inside the species cost and not separable at all**, since
the award record carries no line for a chassis. **Under that reading the data cannot answer the question and the article states that rather than choosing the reading that gives a number.**

### Why Frequent Refresh Is Worth Something, Stated as a Shortfall

**The programme's second claim is about time rather than money and it can be made exact.** The stated
benefit is more opportunities for frequent technology refresh, against a complaint that the time to
initial operating capability for military aircraft has been increasing
\[[GA-ASI first flight release][ref_gaasi_firstflight]] \[[AFRL first flight release][ref_afrl_firstflight]]. **Let the available capability at the frontier grow
exponentially and let a design hold the value it had when it was frozen.**

$$
F(t) \;=\; F_0 \, e^{g t}
$$

**A design frozen at the start of an interval of length $T$ delivers $F_0$ throughout, so the ratio of
what it delivers to what is available is $e^{-g t}$, and the time average of that over the interval has a
closed form.**

$$
\bar{\varrho} \;=\; \frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T} e^{-g t}\,\mathrm{d}t
\;=\; \frac{1 - e^{-g T}}{g T}
$$

$$
\sigma \;=\; 1 - \bar{\varrho} \;=\; 1 - \frac{1 - e^{-gT}}{gT}
$$

**The shortfall $\sigma$ has the right limits and a useful small-argument form.** As $T$ tends to zero it
tends to zero, as $T$ grows without bound it tends to one, and for small $gT$ the expansion is linear in
the freeze interval.

$$
\sigma \;=\; \frac{gT}{2} - \frac{(gT)^{2}}{6} + O\!\bigl((gT)^{3}\bigr)
$$

**Taking a two-year doubling of available capability gives $g$ equal to 0.34657 per year**, from the
relation below, the only place a rate enters.

$$
g \;=\; \frac{\ln 2}{T_2}
$$

| Freeze interval in years | Capability shortfall |
|---:|---:|
| 3 | 37.82 percent |
| 5 | 52.49 percent |
| 10 | 72.05 percent |
| 15 | 80.87 percent |

**The table shows the monotonicity at four values, and the derivative extends the claim to every interval.**

$$
\frac{\partial \sigma}{\partial T}
\;=\; \frac{1 - \bigl(1 + gT\bigr)e^{-gT}}{g T^{2}}
$$

**That is positive for every positive argument, so the shortfall increases with the freeze without
exception.** It is 0.08940 per year at a three-year freeze and 0.01238 at fifteen, a ratio of
7.218, **so the first year of delay costs about seven times what the fifteenth does.** That
is the quantitative form of the programme's argument for cadence.

**For a long freeze the exponential has died and the shortfall has a one-term form.**

$$
\sigma \;\approx\; 1 - \frac{1}{gT}
\qquad\text{for}\qquad
gT \gg 1
$$

**At fifteen years that approximation gives 80.76 percent against the exact
80.87 percent**, so the asymptote is already within a tenth of a point at the longest interval
the table carries.

**Shortening the freeze from fifteen years to three takes the shortfall from 80.87 percent to
37.82 percent, and the gain is the difference.**

$$
\Delta\sigma \;=\; \sigma(T_{\mathrm{old}}) - \sigma(T_{\mathrm{new}})
$$

**That is 43.05 percentage points.** That is the quantitative content of the refresh claim and **it is much larger
than the unit-cost saving commonality buys**, which at the parameters above was
13.63 percent. **If the genus concept is worth what the laboratory says it is worth, the
value is in the cadence rather than in the parts.**

**The doubling time is assumed and nothing in the record supports it.** A different doubling time scales
every figure in the table, and the shortfall for a slowly improving capability is small at any freeze
interval. **The relation is exact and its input is a guess.**

### The Firm on Both Sides of the Gap

**One register fact closes the section and it needs no arithmetic.** The XQ-67A was allocated to General
Atomics on 2023-07-19, and the X-68A, the allocation whose approval made the X-67 unrequestable, was
allocated to General Atomics on 2025-08-20
\[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]]. **The same firm is on both sides of the gap.**

**The register holds sixteen rows for that firm**, being eleven in the unmanned series,
four in the unmanned-control series and one in the research series. **The one research row is the
X-68A.** For scale, the row counts by leading firm name over all 539 rows run 88 for Boeing, 84 for Lockheed, 78 for Raytheon, 33 for Northrop, 24 for Sikorsky and 16 for General Atomics.

**No intent is claimed**, since a firm holding this much of the unmanned register will appear on both
sides of most things, and whether that allocation's requester regarded the 67 as taken is a question the
record does not answer.

## Symbols

**Every symbol the article uses, in one place.** Where two meanings compete for one letter, the
resolution is noted below the table.

| Symbol | Meaning | Unit |
|---|---|---|
| $\mathcal{D}$ | a designation written as an ordered tuple of its fields | none |
| $p$ | status prefix symbol, the experimental letter in this case | none |
| $q$ | modified mission symbol, absent in this case | none |
| $m$ | basic mission symbol, the unmanned letter in this case | none |
| $d$ | design number | none |
| $s$ | design series letter | none |
| $N_d$ | design numbers available within one basic mission | count |
| $N_s$ | series letters available, the alphabet less two | count |
| $N_c$ | distinct number-and-series codes within one basic mission | count |
| $d_k$ | the design number of the $k$th allocation | none |
| $\nu_k$ | the number a sequential allocator would issue at the $k$th allocation | none |
| $\nu^{\mathrm{hi}}_t$ | one above the highest number allocated before a date | none |
| $\nu^{\mathrm{seq}}_t$ | the lowest number never allocated before a date | none |
| $\nu^{\mathrm{rec}}_t$ | one above the most recently allocated number | none |
| $j$ | an allocation index earlier than the $k$th | none |
| $\delta_k$ | the departure of an allocation from the sequential number | count |
| $\Delta_k$ | the increment between consecutive new design numbers | count |
| $\alpha_k$ | the advance of the pointer at the $k$th first allocation | count |
| $\theta$ | the share of pointer advances equal to one | dimensionless |
| $\theta_{\Delta}$ | the share of increments equal to one | dimensionless |
| $A$ | the set of design numbers absent from a series' span | none |
| $\gamma_A$ | absent numbers as a share of the span | dimensionless |
| $\lambda$ | allocations per unit time in one basic mission | reciprocal year |
| $\Delta t_A$ | the absent numbers expressed as the series' own output | year |
| $I$ | information a chosen number can carry at a given ceiling | bit |
| $H$ | entropy of the observed departures | bit |
| $\pi_i$ | the probability of the $i$th departure value | dimensionless |
| $I_{\Sigma}$ | the total information the departures could carry | bit |
| $\mathcal{N}$ | the set of mission-and-number pairs the register carries | none |
| $\mathcal{N}_{>1}$ | those pairs carrying more than one register row | none |
| $\mathcal{M}$ | those pairs naming more than one leading firm | none |
| $\mathcal{R}$ | the register's rows, partitioned by officiality | none |
| $\mathcal{A}_t$ | design numbers allocated in a basic mission before a date | none |
| $\mathcal{B}$ | the article's reference definitions, partitioned by provenance | none |
| $\mathcal{T}$ | the set of official statements of purpose | none |
| $\mathcal{F}$ | the set of leading firm names | none |
| $x$, $y$ | elements of $\mathcal{N}$, being mission-and-number pairs | none |
| $w$ | a single register row | none |
| $\pi$ | per-draw hit probability under the null model | dimensionless |
| $\bar{\tau}$ | mean count of next-in-series values falling inside the band | count |
| $B$ | the width of the band the null model draws from | count |
| $N_h$ | the number of accidental hits under the null model | count |
| $n$ | the number of out-of-sequence allocations tested | count |
| $k$ | the observed number of hits, used as the tail threshold | count |
| $S$ | the number of species raised on one genus | count |
| $P$ | parts in one species if it were designed alone | count |
| $C$ | parts common across the family | count |
| $U$ | distinct parts the family holds in total | count |
| $\phi$ | shared fraction of a species, equal to $C$ over $P$ | dimensionless |
| $\rho$ | distinct parts as a fraction of the bespoke total | dimensionless |
| $Q$ | cumulative production quantity | count |
| $c$ | unit cost at a given cumulative quantity | dollar |
| $c_1$ | unit cost of the first article | dollar |
| $r$ | progress ratio, the cost factor per doubling of quantity | dimensionless |
| $b$ | learning exponent, minus the base-two logarithm of the progress ratio | dimensionless |
| $\kappa$ | fleet-average unit cost factor against bespoke designs | dimensionless |
| $a$ | sharing slope, equal to $S$ to the minus $b$ less one, always negative | dimensionless |
| $\eta$ | sharing penalty coefficient on delivered capability | dimensionless |
| $\phi^{\ast}$ | the shared fraction minimising effective cost | dimensionless |
| $\mathcal{C}_g$ | non-recurring cost of developing the genus | dollar |
| $c_s$ | non-recurring cost of one species on the genus | dollar |
| $c_b$ | non-recurring cost of one wholly bespoke aircraft type | dollar |
| $S^{\ast}$ | species at which the genus repays its own development cost | count |
| $\varsigma_i$ | one award as a share of a set of awards | dimensionless |
| $\epsilon$ | relative deviation of an outturn from a reference figure | dimensionless |
| $f$ | the position of an event within an interval | dimensionless |
| $\Theta$ | discriminant of the first-order condition for the optimum | dimensionless |
| $\eta_{\ast}$ | the sharing penalty above which an interior optimum exists | dimensionless |
| $F$ | capability available at the frontier at a given time | capability |
| $F_0$ | capability available when a design is frozen | capability |
| $g$ | exponential growth rate of available capability | reciprocal year |
| $t$ | time since a design was frozen | year |
| $T$ | the interval a design is held frozen between refreshes | year |
| $T_2$ | doubling time of available capability | year |
| $\bar{\varrho}$ | time-average ratio of delivered to available capability | dimensionless |
| $\sigma$ | time-average capability shortfall, one less the ratio above | dimensionless |
| $K$ | research records the survey admits | count |
| $\varpi$ | report primaries as a share of the admitted records | dimensionless |
| $\mathcal{K}_c$ | the records whose titles carry one cluster's characteristic phrases | none |
| $\bar{m}$ | mean clusters whose phrases an admitted title carries | count |

**The collisions, and what yielded.** The sequential number takes $\nu_k$ so that the costs $c_s$ and
$c_b$ keep the cost letter. The status prefix keeps $p$ and the null model's probability takes $\pi$. The
common part count keeps $C$, so the genus cost and the cluster set take calligraphic letters. The freeze interval
keeps $T$ and the null model's mean target count takes $\bar{\tau}$. The discriminant takes $\Theta$
because $\Delta$ is a difference throughout, and a row inside the well-definedness condition takes $w$
because $r$ is the progress ratio. **$K$ counts the records the research survey admits while $k$ indexes
an allocation, so the case is doing work.** The distinct-part ratio $\rho$ and the capability ratio
$\bar{\varrho}$ render similarly at small sizes and never share an expression.

**Capability has no unit**, because the refresh derivation needs only the ratio of two capabilities, and
**a designation's fields have none**, because they are symbols in a code, and only for that reason can a
design number be chosen rather than measured.

## The Contemporary Literature

| Cluster | Records | What it holds |
|---|---:|---|
| Commonality | 207 | component commonality and its indices, its effect on inventory and assembly, and standardisation in production |
| Product families | 298 | product families and platforms, platform-based design, mass customisation, software product lines |
| Modularity | 161 | modular product architecture, design structure matrices, the theory of modularity, dominant designs |
| Open architecture | 47 | the modular open systems approach, open architectures in defence acquisition, integrated modular avionics |
| Cost of variety | 56 | learning curves, cost-estimating relationships and cost growth, economies of scope, the cost of variety |
| Flexibility | 28 | real options in engineering design, technology insertion, obsolescence and diminishing manufacturing sources |
| Crewed and uncrewed teaming | 81 | manned-unmanned teaming, collaborative combat aircraft, unmanned air combat |
| Attritability | 4 | attritable, low-cost and semi-expendable unmanned aircraft |
| Autonomy | 14 | autonomy architecture, human-autonomy teaming and trust, tactical datalinks, interoperability |
| Configuration identification and nomenclature | 59 | configuration identification and management, engineering change, nomenclature standards |
| Identifiers | 63 | persistent identifiers, the digital object identifier, object identifier registries, namespaces, semantic versioning |
| Numerical taxonomy | 24 | the method and theory of numerical taxonomy |
| Unmanned aircraft design | 19 | conceptual design and sizing of unmanned aircraft |
| Demonstrators | 24 | X-plane and other flight demonstrators, rapid acquisition, technology readiness levels |
| Digital engineering | 24 | model-based systems engineering, virtual prototyping, digital twins, requirements engineering |
| **Total** | **1,109** | |

**The survey's most useful finding is that this article's literature is not an aeronautical literature.**
The X-67 is a number, the aeroplane that consumed it was built to prove a method of
building aeroplanes, and the method has a large, quantitative and continuously active literature under
product families, component commonality and modular architecture. **That literature lives in mechanical engineering and management science, so most of the survey comes from a journal index rather than from a report archive.**

**The survey's 1,109 research records fall across fifteen clusters, one cluster to each record.** A
record's title is matched against the characteristic phrases of every cluster, and the record is filed in
the first cluster in table order whose phrases its title contains, except two titles on product-line
requirements engineering, which the requirements phrase alone would file under digital engineering and
which belong with the software product lines under product families. A title may contain the phrases of
more than one cluster, and the mean number of clusters a title matches says how often.

$$
\bar{m} \;=\; \frac{1}{K} \sum_{c} \lvert \mathcal{K}_c \rvert
$$

**That is 1.0649 clusters per record across 1,181 matches, and 72 titles match
two.** A mean barely above one says the clusters are nearly disjoint, **and that reflects the admission rule rather than the field**,
since each cluster admits on compound nouns the others do not
use.

**The shape of that table is the article's subject restated.** The three largest clusters are
product families at 298, commonality at 207 and modularity at
161, which together are the engineering literature of when a thing is a new thing. **The
configuration identification and identifier clusters, at 59 and 63, are the
literature of how engineered items and records are identified and named.** The numerical taxonomy cluster
at 24 is the literature of classifying at all.

**The smallest cluster is attritability at four records, deliberately**, because attritability is the
[X-58][related_post_a355_x58_slot_taken_by_xq58] article's keystone and that article surveyed it, as noted under
Out of Scope.

**Two clusters hold literatures outside product design, and both are deliberate.** The identifier
cluster reaches persistent identifier systems, object identifier registries, namespace definitions and
software versioning, because the questions a registry faces about allocating and retiring names are the
questions the designation system faces and the answers are better documented. **The numerical taxonomy
cluster reaches the method of numerical classification, because the programme chose a biological metaphor
and the discipline it borrowed from has a worked method for deciding what counts as a kind.**

**The oldest records in the survey show that standardisation is a far older subject than the designation
system, and that naming entered the research record later.** The survey reaches a paper on the
standardisation of electrical engineering plant from 1900 and its discussion \[[Sellon 1900][research_sellon_1900]\]
\[[Crompton et al. 1900][research_crompton_blackburn_1900]\], and a run of papers
and section discussions on standardisation as applied to production engineering from 1933
\[[Standardisation as Applied 1933][research_standardisation_as_1933]\]
\[[Wright 1933][research_wright_1933]\].
**The question of how to standardise manufactured things is older than the designation system this
article is about by two generations**, and the engineering literature treated it as a subject in its own
right before the first aeroplane flew. **The question of how to name what engineers make reaches the
survey's research records in 1962**, with a compilation of aerodynamic nomenclature and axes systems
\[[Wright 1962][research_wright_1962]\], the year in which the designator format was established \[[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]\].

### Commonality

**The sharing of parts and its measures.** Commonality indices, component sharing across a product line, the inventory and assembly consequences of a shared part, and the standardisation of production, its older relative. **39 of these titles name standardisation and 30 concern inventory, stock or assemble-to-order systems**, because a part shared across products pools the demand for it.

### Product families

**The design of families of products on a shared platform.** Platform selection and optimisation, commonality and variety trade-offs, mass customisation and the management of product variety, with 31 titles on software product lines, the same idea in a discipline that builds variants from shared code.

### Modularity

**The architecture of a product divided into modules.** Module identification, modular product architecture, the theory of modularity and its option value, and the dominant-design and architectural-innovation work that explains when an architecture becomes fixed. **65 of these titles name the design structure matrix**, which is the field's principal instrument for deciding where a module boundary falls.
